Webs and multiwebs for the symplectic group
Richard Kenyon, Haihan Wu

Abstract
We define 2 ​ n 2n -multiwebs on planar graphs and discuss their relation with Sp ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) \mathrm{Sp}(2n) -webs. On a planar graph with a symplectic local system we define a matrix whose Pfaffian is the sum of traces of 2 ​ n 2n -multiwebs. As application we generalize Kasteleyn’s theorem from dimer covers to 2 ​ n 2n -multiweb covers of planar graphs with U ⁡ ( n ) U(n) gauge group. For Sp ⁡ ( 4 ) \mathrm{Sp}(4) we relate Kuperberg’s “tetravalent vertex” to the determinant, and classify reduced 4 4 -webs on some simple surfaces: the annulus, torus, and pair of pants. We likewise define, for Sp ⁡ ( 2 ​ n ) \mathrm{Sp}(2n) and q = 1 q=1 , a 2 ​ n 2n -valent vertex corresponding to the determinant, and classify reduced 2 ​ n 2n -webs on an annulus.



1
Introduction

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(V,E)
be a planar graph with
N
vertices.
An
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-connection
\Phi
on
{\mathcal{G}}
, also known as an

\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-local system
, is the choice,
for each directed edge
e=uv
, of
an element
\phi_{uv}\in\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, so that
\phi_{vu}=(\phi_{uv})^{-1}
.

A
2n
-multiweb
in
{\mathcal{G}}
(see
[
DKS22
]
) is a function
m:E\to\{0,1,\dots,2n\}
, summing to
2n
at every vertex:

\sum_{u\sim v}m_{uv}=2n
for all
v\in V
. We call
m_{e}
the
multiplicity
of edge
e
in
m
.
For example a
2
-multiweb is (ignoring edges of multiplicity zero) a disjoint collection of simple loops and doubled edges.
We let
\Omega_{2n}=\Omega_{2n}({\mathcal{G}})
be the set of all
2n
-multiwebs in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Multiwebs are variants of
webs
, which are certain types of graphs used in the study of the representation theory of linear groups,
in particular
\mathrm{SL}(n)
and
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, see
[
Kup96
,
Lam15
]
. To each multiweb is associated a corresponding web.

Each
2n
-multiweb
m\in\Omega_{2n}
has a
trace
, depending on the connection
\Phi
, defined below.
Our main result, Theorem
4
, is the construction of a modified weighted adjacency matrix
\tilde{H}=\tilde{H}_{\Phi}
of
{\mathcal{G}}
whose Pfaffian is the sum of traces of all

2n
-multiwebs of
{\mathcal{G}}
. An analogous result for the Lie group
\mathrm{SL}(n)
for bipartite graphs
{\mathcal{G}}
was recently obtained in
[
DKS22
]
.

As application, to a graph
{\mathcal{G}}
embedded in a planar domain
\Sigma
we associate a
regular function
\mathrm{Pf}(\tilde{H})
on the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-character variety of
\Sigma
which,
encodes information about flat
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
local systems on
{\mathcal{G}}
. One can use these functions
to study the geometry of the character variety,
but also (as we do here) the combinatorics
and probability theory of random multiwebs in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

The cases
n=1
and
n=2
, that is, for the groups
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
and
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
, are of special combinatorial interest.

1.1
Kasteleyn’s theorem

For
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
we have some concrete combinatorial applications to enumeration of perfect matchings.
A
2
-multiweb is
topologically very simple: it is just a disjoint collection of simple loops and doubled edges.
Theorem
4
in this setting can be used to study the dimer model and double dimer model.
Kasteleyn’s celebrated theorem
[
Kas63
]
counts dimer covers (perfect matchings) of a planar graph via the Pfaffian of a modified (antisymmetric) adjacency matrix.
We give an alternate formulation and proof here (Theorem
6
) using the above
2
-multiweb trace enumeration, with an appropriate

Kasteleyn connection
on
{\mathcal{G}}
.

By using our
2n
-multiweb enumeration and a higher-rank version of the Kasteleyn connection we
generalize Kasteleyn’s theorem to an enumeration of
2n
-dimer covers interacting through the group
U(n)

(see Section
5
and Theorem
7
).

1.2
The
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category

Theorem
4
, our main result, inspires a change of viewpoint on the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category,
since it highlights the importance of the tetravalent vertex.
Kuperberg
[
Kup96
]
defined
the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category
by generators and relations (Definition
1
below),
where one of the relations is
\frac{\pi}{2}
-rotational invariant:

and he deduced skein relations with tetravalent vertices instead of trivalent vertices (Proposition
12
below).

In order to study the connection between
4
-webs (that is, webs with only tetravalent vertices)
and
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-webs, we observe in the following theorem that the tetravalent vertex corresponds to the determinant of a
4
by
4
matrix, i.e. the exterior product of
4
vectors in the
4
-dimensional defining representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
.

Theorem 1
.

Denote
\textbf{det}:=\hbox to52.6pt{\vbox to37.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.30144pt\lower-34.93526pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 28.67 -14.34 35.84 -33.45 35.84 -47.79 M 0 0 C 7.17 -14.34 11.95 -33.45 11.95 -47.79 M 0 0 C -7.17 -14.34 -11.95 -33.45 -11.95 -47.79 M 0 0 C -28.67 -14.34 -35.84 -33.45 -35.84 -47.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \in{\Hom}(\ObjOne^{\otimes 4},\mathbb{R})
,
We have :

\displaystyle\Phi(\textbf{det}):\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ V^{\otimes 4}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes v_{3}\otimes v_{4}\mapsto

\displaystyle|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}\wedge v_{4}|

Denote
\textbf{codet}:=\hbox to52.6pt{\vbox to37.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.30144pt\lower-2.50754pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 28.67 14.34 35.84 33.45 35.84 47.79 M 0 0 C 7.17 14.34 11.95 33.45 11.95 47.79 M 0 0 C -7.17 14.34 -11.95 33.45 -11.95 47.79 M 0 0 C -28.67 14.34 -35.84 33.45 -35.84 47.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in{\Hom}(\mathbb{R},\ObjOne^{\otimes 4})
,
We have :

\Phi(\textbf{codet}):\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow V^{\otimes 4}

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 1\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{4}}(-1)^{\sigma}e_{\sigma(1)}\otimes e_{\sigma(2)}\otimes e_{\sigma(3)}\otimes e_{\sigma(4)}

For the proof of Theorem
1
see Section
6.4
.
Although we will not use it here, we give a quantum version (for the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sp}_{4})
) of Theorem
1
in the Appendix, Section
A.2
.

If our graph
{\mathcal{G}}
is drawn on a multiply connected domain
in
{\mathbb{C}}
with flat connection, these
4
-web skein relations, which preserve the trace, allow us to replace each

4
-web with a linear combination of

reduced webs
: a
4
-web on a surface is said to be
reduced
if it has no contractible faces of degree
0,1,2
or
3
(see
[
Kup96
]
).
The weighted enumeration of
4
-multiwebs becomes a weighted
enumeration of reduced
4
-webs.
We carry out this enumeration procedure for the simplest case of an annulus. See Section
7
.
For the next simplest surfaces, a torus and a pair of pants, we classify reduced webs and give some basic results (Sections

7.2
and
7.3
).

1.3
The
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
case

The definition of the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category was generalized to the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
case for any rank
n
in
[
BERT21
]
. As above we define
a
2n
-valent vertex (Definition
27
) in the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web category with the help of the braiding structure, so that the vertex corresponds to the determinant of a
2n
by
2n
matrix.

Theorem 2
.

Denote
\textbf{det}:=\hbox to69.87pt{\vbox to36.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.93526pt\lower-34.93526pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 40.62 -14.34 47.79 -33.45 47.79 -47.79 M 0 0 C 19.11 -14.34 23.89 -33.45 23.89 -47.79 M 0 0 C -19.11 -14.34 -23.89 -33.45 -23.89 -47.79 M 0 0 C -40.62 -14.34 -47.79 -33.45 -47.79 -47.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.56 -43.01 M -8.72 -43.01 C -8.72 -42.54 -9.09 -42.17 -9.56 -42.17 C -10.02 -42.17 -10.4 -42.54 -10.4 -43.01 C -10.4 -43.47 -10.02 -43.85 -9.56 -43.85 C -9.09 -43.85 -8.72 -43.47 -8.72 -43.01 Z M -9.56 -43.01 M 0 -43.01 M 0.84 -43.01 C 0.84 -42.54 0.46 -42.17 0 -42.17 C -0.46 -42.17 -0.84 -42.54 -0.84 -43.01 C -0.84 -43.47 -0.46 -43.85 0 -43.85 C 0.46 -43.85 0.84 -43.47 0.84 -43.01 Z M 0 -43.01 M 9.56 -43.01 M 10.4 -43.01 C 10.4 -42.54 10.02 -42.17 9.56 -42.17 C 9.09 -42.17 8.72 -42.54 8.72 -43.01 C 8.72 -43.47 9.09 -43.85 9.56 -43.85 C 10.02 -43.85 10.4 -43.47 10.4 -43.01 Z M 9.56 -43.01}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.71 M 3.6 -1.71 C 3.6 0.28 1.99 1.89 0 1.89 C -1.99 1.89 -3.6 0.28 -3.6 -1.71 C -3.6 -3.69 -1.99 -5.31 0 -5.31 C 1.99 -5.31 3.6 -3.69 3.6 -1.71 Z M 0 -1.71}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\Hom(\ObjOne^{\otimes 2n},\mathbb{R})
. Then

\displaystyle\Phi(\textbf{det}):\qquad\qquad\qquad{V_{\varpi_{1}}}^{\otimes 2n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n}

\displaystyle\mapsto|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdot\cdot\cdot\wedge v_{2n}|,

where for
w\in\bigwedge^{2n}V_{\varpi_{1}}
, we denote by
|w|
the element
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
w=r(e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge\cdot\cdot\cdot\wedge e_{2n})
.

For the proof see Section
8.2
.
This construction allows us to relate
2n
-multiwebs to
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
webs.
Using this we give an explicit classification of reduced
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
webs on an annulus in Theorem
29
.

Although we won’t use it here, a version of the degree-
n
vertex and Theorem
2
for the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n})
is given in the Appendix, Section
A.1
. See
[
CD24
]
for an alternate formulation.
We don’t have, at this moment, the analogous result in the quantum symplectic case.

This research was supported by NSF grant DMS-1940932 and CCF-2317280 and the Simons Foundation grant 327929 and 994328.
We thank Elijah Bodish, Daniel Douglas, Joel Hass, Vijay Higgins, Greg Kuperberg, Nicholas Ovenhouse and Sri Tata for discussions and insights.

2
A history of web categories

The discovery of the Jones polynomial
[
Jon85
]
triggered significant mathematical developments in
many areas including knot theory and quantum algebra. The Jones polynomial is a link invariant, written as a Laurent polynomial in one variable
q
.
It was discovered by Reshetikhin and Turaev
[
RT91
]
that the Jones polynomial can be defined by using the braiding structure in the ribbon category of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. This ribbon category is universally constructed for any simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, giving a generalization of the Jones polynomial to a family of quantum link invariants,
one for each simple Lie algebra. When
q
is at a root of unity,
these ribbon categories also give invariants of a three-manifold by coloring the framed link along which Dehn surgery
is performed.

The ribbon category that computes the Jones polynomial can be presented as the Temperley-Lieb category
[
TL71
]
, which is equivalent to the subcategory of
\Rep(U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}))
generated by the
q
-analogue of the defining representation. Hence we can use diagrams and graphical calculations in the Temperley-Lieb category to study the representation theory of quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
.

The web category of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
was first introduced by Kuperberg
[
Kup94
]
to compute the quantum
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
invariants for links and knots. The definition of web categories was later generalized to include all the rank two simple Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
, that is
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
,
\mathfrak{sp}_{4}
, or
\mathfrak{g}_{2}

[
Kup96
]
. It was shown there that the web category of
\mathfrak{g}
is equivalent to the category of fundamental representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, generalizing the relation between the Temperley-Lieb category and the representations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
.

Bearing in mind the goal of giving graphically presented generators and relations descriptions of the categories of fundamental representations of quantum groups, the definition of web categories was later extended to types A
[
CKM14
]
and C
[
BERT21
]
.
Web categories are widely used to tackle problems in quantum topology, including
the categorification of quantum link invariants generalizing Khovanov homology
[
Kho00
]
,
and giving formulas for irreducible representations of quantum groups generalizing the Jones-Wenzl projectors
[
Jon83
,
Wen87
]
.

The web category of
\text{GL}_{n}
was related to the dimer model in
[
DKS22
]
.
We extend here these recent results to
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
when q=1.
For recent work on dimers and
\Rep(U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}))

see
[
DKO+24
]
.

3
Multiwebs, cilia, traces

3.1
Symplectic group

The Lie group
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)=\mathrm{Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
, the
symplectic group
, is the set of matrices in
\mathrm{SL}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
preserving the standard symplectic form

\mathrm{Sp}(2n)=\{M\in\mathrm{SL}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})~|~M^{t}JM=J\},

where
J
is the matrix

J=\begin{pmatrix}0_{n}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0_{n}\end{pmatrix}

and
I_{n},0_{n}
are the
n\times n
identity matrix and zero matrix, respectively.
The operator
J
defines the standard symplectic form
\omega
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
via
\omega(u,v)=u^{t}Jv
.

3.2
Cilia and orientations

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(V,E)
be a planar graph with an
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
connection
\Phi=\{\phi_{uv}\}_{uv\in E}
.
The definition of trace of a multiweb on
{\mathcal{G}}

requires some extra combinatorial data associated to
{\mathcal{G}}
. We need an
orientation
of the edges of
{\mathcal{G}}

and a choice of
cilia
.
By cilia we mean the following. At each vertex of
{\mathcal{G}}
choose a total order of the incident edges, which is compatible with the counterclockwise circular order.
To do this it suffices to choose a starting edge. Notationally we put a mark, called cilium, in the wedge between the
first edge and last edge, at each vertex. See Figure
2
.

The edge orientations and cilia choices can be arbitrary; different choices affect the definition of trace of any given multiweb by a sign.
However to get coherence of signs when summing traces of different multiwebs, we need to impose restrictions on both the orientations and the choice of cilia. One way is as follows.
Given a generic planar embedding of
{\mathcal{G}}
, orient the edges upwards according to the
y
-coordinate of their endpoints.
At each vertex, put the cilium horizontally to its left. We call this the
standard choice

of cilia and orientations.
See an example in Figure
2
.

3.3
Trace

Let
m
be a multiweb in
{\mathcal{G}}
. The trace
\mathrm{Tr}(m)
is defined as follows.
First assume
m
is
simple
, that is,
m_{e}=0
or
m_{e}=1
for every edge.
At a vertex
v
, let
vv_{1},\dots,vv_{2n}
be the adjacent edges in the support of
m
, in counterclockwise
order starting from the cilium.
Let
W_{i}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
be a vector space associated to the half-edge of
vv_{i}
near
v
.
Let
\{e^{i}_{1},\dots,e^{i}_{2n}\}
be a standard basis of
W_{i}
(we will drop the superscripts in the future). We
define a vector
\gamma_{v}\in W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{2n}
,
the
codeterminant
, via the formula

\gamma_{v}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2n}}(-1)^{\sigma}e_{\sigma(1)}\otimes e_{\sigma(2)}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{\sigma(2n)}.

The trace of
m
is now the contraction of the tensor product of the
\gamma_{v}
along edges using the connection and symplectic form:
In the tensor product
\bigotimes_{v}\gamma_{v}
, the tensor components for the two halves of edge
v\to v^{\prime}

are contracted using
J\phi_{vv^{\prime}}
. That is, for a simple tensor
\dots\otimes a_{v}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\dots

where
a_{v},a_{v^{\prime}}
correspond to the two halves of edge
vv^{\prime}
, directed from
v
to
v^{\prime}

the component of the contraction is
(a_{v^{\prime}})^{t}J\phi_{vv^{\prime}}a_{v}
.
Note that if we reverse the orientation of the edge
vv^{\prime}
, the contribution from this edge changes sign:

a_{v}^{t}J\phi_{v^{\prime}v}a_{v^{\prime}}=-(a_{v^{\prime}})^{t}J\phi_{vv^{\prime}}a_{v}

see (
7
) below.

If
m
is not simple, for each edge
e=vv^{\prime}
of multiplicity
m_{e}>1
and connection matrix
\phi_{vv^{\prime}}
,
replace
e
by
m_{e}
parallel edges, with the same endpoints
v,v^{\prime}
, same orientation, and with same connection matrix

\phi_{vv^{\prime}}
. This splitting results in a simple multiweb
m^{*}

on a new graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
. We define

\mathrm{Tr}(m):=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(m^{*})}{\prod_{e}m_{e}!}.

(1)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

Note that changing the cilium at a single vertex by
one notch, crossing edge
e
, changes the sign of the trace of
m
by
(-1)^{m_{e}}
.
Changing the orientation of an edge of multiplicity
m_{e}
changes the sign of the trace by

(-1)^{m_{e}}
.

3.4
Trace for bipartite graphs

Proposition 1
.

If
{\mathcal{G}}
is bipartite, and the edges are oriented from black to white,
the above definition of trace agrees with the
\mathrm{SL}(2n)
trace
(up to a global sign independent of
m
).

Note that in
[
Sik01
,
DKS22
]
the convention for the
\mathrm{SL}(2n)
trace is to use a clockwise orientation
of half-edges at white vertices and counterclockwise orientation at black vertices.
For this proof we use the counterclockwise orientation at both types of vertices; this is the
trace convention as defined in Kuperberg
[
Kup96
]
. (Changing cyclic orientation from clockwise to counterclockwise
multiplies the codeterminant
at that vertex, and thus the trace of any multiweb, by
(-1)^{n}
. So the net effect is to multiply the sum of traces by

(-1)^{n|W|}
where
|W|
is the number of white vertices.)

Proof.

Let
m
be a simple multiweb. At each white vertex, apply the operator
(-J)^{\otimes 2n}

to the codeterminant. On the one hand this has no effect since the codeterminant is invariant under

\mathrm{SL}(2n)
change of basis, and
-J\in\mathrm{SL}(2n)
.
On the other hand this has the effect of removing the
J
’s from the edges, that is,
the edge contribution
(a_{v^{\prime}})^{t}J\phi_{vv^{\prime}}a_{v}
becomes
(a_{v^{\prime}})^{t}\phi_{vv^{\prime}}a_{v}
,
which is the edge contribution for the
\mathrm{SL}(2n)
trace.
∎

3.5
Trace for the identity connection

The trace of a multiweb for the identity connection can be defined in terms of edge colorings, as follows.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}=\{1,2,\dots,2n\}
be the set of colors. Let
\sigma:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be the involution switching
i
and
i+n
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. We refer to
\sigma
as
complementation
.

An
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-coloring
of
m
is defined as follows.
To an edge
e

of multiplicity
m_{e}
we associate two complementary subsets
S_{e},T_{e}
of
{\mathcal{C}}
, each of size
m_{e}
, one associated with each half-edge of
e
. At each vertex the color subsets on the adjacent half-edges are required to partition
{\mathcal{C}}
. See Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
An
\mathrm{Sp}(6)
-coloring of edges in a
6
-multiweb. Edge multiplicity is indicated by thickness.

We claim that the trace of
m
is the signed sum of such
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-colorings.
Let us first suppose
m
is simple:
m_{e}=0
or
1
for every edge.
Given a coloring
c
, at each vertex
v
, starting from the cilia and going counterclockwise
the order of the colors encountered (ignoring edges with
m_{e}=0
) is a permutation of
\{1,2,\dots,2n\}
and thus
has a signature,
c_{v}
. For every edge

uv
with
m_{e}=1
, using the assigned orientation we have a sign
c_{e}
which is
+1
if the colors are increasing
(that is,
i,i+n
) and
-1
if decreasing (that is,
i+n,i
).
The sign of the coloring
c
, denoted
(-1)^{c}
, is defined to be the
product of these vertex signs and edge signs:

(-1)^{c}=\prod_{v}c_{v}\prod_{e}c_{e}.

Finally

\mathrm{Tr}(m):=\sum_{c}(-1)^{c}

(2)

where the sum is over edge colorings.

If
m
is not simple, for each edge
e
of multiplicity
m_{e}>1
, replace
e
with
m_{e}
parallel edges,
with the same orientation, to get a simple web
m^{*}
on a new graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
. Then

\mathrm{Tr}(m):=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(m^{*})}{\prod_{e}m_{e}!}.

(3)

Theorem 3
.

For the identity connection, the definition of trace in Equations (
2
) and (
3
) agrees with the tensor network definition in Section
3.3
.

Proof.

We identify the standard basis vectors of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
with the
2n
colors, so that color
i
corresponds to
e_{i}
.
A codeterminant then corresponds to all possible choices of ways to color the half-edges out of a vertex,
along with a sign given by the signature
c_{v}
. For the identity connection,
a coloring at one vertex pairs nontrivially with a coloring at an adjacent
vertex only if the colors on the common edge are complementary, and in that case the pairing along the edge is

c_{e}
: The edge sign corresponds to the operation of
J
on basis vectors:
e_{i+n}^{t}Je_{i}=1
corresponds to color

i,i+n
having sign
+1
, and
e_{i}^{t}Je_{i+n}=-1
corresponds to color

i+n,i
having sign
-1
.
∎

See an example in Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
For
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
, this graph has
8
multiwebs (two are shown, in blue). For the orientation and the cilia shown,
the traces are
+2
for the first and
-2
for the second.

There is generally no coherence in the signs of different colorings, that is, terms in (
2
) do not all have the same sign,
so the absolute value of the trace is not equal to the number of colorings.
See however Section
5

where sign coherence can be obtained by tensoring with a “Kasteleyn connection”.

For a general
Sp(2n)
connection we have the following trace formula.

Proposition 2
.

Given
\mathcal{G}
with an
Sp(2n)
connection
\Phi=\{\phi_{uv}\}_{uv\in E}
, and standard cilia and edge orientations, for a simple multiweb
m
we have

\mathrm{Tr}(m):=\sum_{c}\prod_{v}c_{v}\prod_{uv}(e_{c,vu})^{t}J\phi_{uv}e_{c,uv},

(4)

where the sum is over all colorings of half edges of
m
such that all colors in
\mathcal{C}
are used around each vertex, the first product is over vertices
v
and the second is over oriented edges
uv
,
e_{c,vu}
corresponds to the color of the half edge of
uv
adjacent to
v
in coloring
c
, and
e_{c,uv}
corresponds to the color of the half edge of
uv
adjacent to
u
in coloring
c
.

If
m
is not simple, for each edge
e
of multiplicity
m_{e}>1
, replace
e
with
m_{e}
parallel edges,
with the same orientation, to get a simple web
m^{*}
on a new graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
. Then

\mathrm{Tr}(m):=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(m^{*})}{\prod_{e}m_{e}!}.

(5)

Proof.

This works as in the case of the identity connection (Theorem
3
), except that color sets
S_{e},T_{e}
are not necessarily complementary: when the identity connection is replaced with a general
Sp(2n)
connection
\Phi
, the edge contribution to
\mathrm{Tr}(m)
(for a simple
2n
-web) is changed from
(e_{c,vu})^{t}Je_{c,uv}
to
(e_{c,vu})^{t}J\phi_{uv}e_{c,uv}
.
∎

3.6
2
-multiweb traces

Proposition 3
.

For a planar graph
{\mathcal{G}}
with
N
vertices, with an
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
-connection
\Phi
, and
for a choice of standard orientation and cilia, the trace of a
2
-multiweb
m
is

\mathrm{Tr}(m)=(-1)^{c_{1}+N}\prod_{\gamma}(-\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{\gamma})),

(6)

where
c_{1}
is the number of doubled edges, the product is over nontrivial loops
\gamma
of
m
,
\phi_{\gamma}
is the
monodromy of
\Phi
along
\gamma
(with either orientation) and
\mathrm{tr}
is the matrix trace.

Proof.

Let us first consider the case where
\Phi
is the identity connection.
It suffices to assume
m
is simple, so there are no doubled edges, by (
3
).
Let
\gamma
be a loop, oriented counterclockwise (cclw), of length
L
. Color each edge of
\gamma
with colors
1,2
in
cclw order (the first half edge has color
1
, the second half color
2
).
There are two sources of signs: from the cilia and from the edge signs.
The contribution to the sign from the edges of
\gamma
is
-1
per descending edge, that is,
edge where the second vertex has lower
y
coordinate than the first.
The contribution to the sign from the cilia of
\gamma
is
-1
per cilium along
\gamma

which is in the region enclosed by
\gamma
, that is,
-1
per pair of adjacent ascending edges,

-1
per local minima traversed right to left, and
-1
per local maxima traversed left to right
(see Figure
3
).

Figure 3:
This loop has
s=3
interior cilia and
d=4
descending edges (when oriented cclw) marked in blue, for a total sign of
(-1)^{7}=-1
.

By Lemma
1
below,
\gamma

contributes a net sign of
(-1)^{L+1}
. If we reverse the colors, so that each edge is colored
2,1
in cclw order,
the reversal induces a sign change at each edge and each vertex, so no net sign change.
Thus including both colorings of
\gamma
,
the contribution to the trace is
2(-1)^{L+1}
.

Taking the product over all components the sign is
(-2)^{c_{2}}(-1)^{N}
.

Now assume
\Phi
is a general
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
connection.
Let
\gamma
be a loop
v_{1},\dots,v_{L}
, traversed cclw.
Let
A_{i}=\phi_{v_{i}v_{i+1}}
be the connection matrix on edge
v_{i}v_{i+1}
oriented from
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
.
We reverse the standard orientation on edges that are downward-pointing along
\gamma
,
so that edges
are oriented consistently along
\gamma
. This reversal introduces a sign
(-1)^{d}
,
where
d
is the number of downward-oriented edges along
\gamma
.

Assume for the moment
that all cilia along
\gamma
are external to
\gamma
. Then the codeterminant at
v_{i}
is

w_{i}:=e_{1}^{i+1}\otimes e_{2}^{i}-e_{2}^{i+1}\otimes e_{1}^{i}

(with cyclic indices).

The contribution from
\gamma
to the trace is then by definition the contraction

(JA_{L})w_{L}(JA_{L-1})w_{L-1}\dots(JA_{1})w_{1}.

Reparenthesizing and using cyclicity, this is the contraction

A_{L}(w_{L}J)A_{L-1}(w_{L-1}J)\dots JA_{1}(w_{1}J).

At
v_{i+1}
we thus apply the corresponding
J
along edge
v_{i}v_{i+1}
to the codeterminant
at
v_{i+1}
, yielding

w_{i}J=e_{1}^{i+1}\otimes(Je_{2}^{i})-e_{2}^{i+1}\otimes(Je_{1}^{i})=e_{1}^{i+1}\otimes e_{1}^{i}+e_{2}^{i+1}\otimes e_{2}^{i}.

Contracting the
A
’s with these tensors corresponds to matrix multiplication; the trace becomes

\mathrm{tr}(A_{L}A_{L-1}\dots A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{\gamma}).

Now consider the case where not all cilia are external.
Each cilium on
\gamma
enclosed by
\gamma
changes the sign of the trace, compared
to the “all external cilia” case.
Taking into account the additional sign contribution from the downward-oriented edges,
as in the case of the identity connection the contribution to the trace is
(-1)^{L+1}\mathrm{tr}(\phi_{\gamma}).
Taking the product over all loops yields the formula.
∎

Lemma 1
.

Let
\gamma
be a closed polygonal path
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}=x_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, possibly with repeated vertices, but
with no horizontal steps.
Put a cilium at every vertex
x_{i}
, to the west of it.
Let
s
be the number of cilia to the left of
\gamma
(that is, for which
\gamma
passes the cilium on its left).
Then the number of downward-pointing edges
d
of
\gamma
satisfies
d\equiv s+n+1\mod 2
.

See Figure
3
for an example.

Proof.

This is true if
\gamma
has a single downward-pointing edge. Starting from this
\gamma
, we deform
\gamma
by moving vertices
x_{i}
continuously in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Both
d
and
s
change by exactly
1

each time an edge of
\gamma
changes from upward-pointing to downwards or vice versa.
∎

4
The
H
matrix

Suppose that
{\mathcal{G}}
is planar with standard orientation and cilia, and with an
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
connection
\Phi=\{\phi_{uv}\}_{uv\in E}
.
We define a matrix
H=H(\Phi)
which does not depend on the orientation or cilia, as follows:

H\in M_{V\times V}(M_{2n})
is the matrix with entries

H_{uv}=\begin{cases}J\phi_{uv}&u\sim v\\
{\bf 0}&\text{else}\end{cases}

where
{\bf 0}
is the
0
matrix in
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
.
Note that we have

H_{uv}=J\phi_{uv}=J\phi_{vu}^{-1}=\phi_{vu}^{t}J=-(J\phi_{vu})^{t}=-(H_{vu})^{t}.

(7)

Let
N=|V|
be the number of vertices of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Let
\tilde{H}
be the
2nN\times 2nN
matrix obtained from
H
by replacing each entry with its
2n\times 2n
array
of reals. Note that
\tilde{H}
is antisymmetric, by (
7
).

The Pfaffian
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}
of an antisymmetric matrix
\tilde{H}
is defined by

\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\tilde{H}_{\sigma(1)\sigma(2)}\dots\tilde{H}_{\sigma(2M-1)\sigma(2M)}

(8)

where the sum is over pairings
\sigma=\{\sigma(1),\sigma(2)\},\{\sigma(3),\sigma(4)\},\dots

of the indices, with
\sigma(2i-1)<\sigma(2i)
for each
i
.

Theorem 4
.

We have
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}=\pm\sum_{m\in\Omega_{2n}}\mathrm{Tr}(m).

Proof.

It is convenient to assume vertices of
{\mathcal{G}}
are in generic position and to index them according to increasing
y
-coordinate.
This ordering does not affect
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:=\{1,\dots,2n\}
. Let
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
have standard basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{2n}
.
Let
M=nN
, so that
\tilde{H}
is a
2M\times 2M
matrix.

We let
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
be the graph with vertices
\tilde{V}=V\times\mathcal{C}
and edges
(u,i)\to(v,j)
for each edge
uv
of
{\mathcal{G}}
,
and
i,j\in\mathcal{C}
. The edge
(u,i)\to(v,j)
is given edge weight
e_{j}^{t}J\phi_{uv}e_{i}
.

Consider the expression
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}
of (
8
). Note that by our choice of vertex indexing in terms of increasing
y
coordinate,
\tilde{H}_{\sigma(2i-1),\sigma(2i)}
is now ordered according to the standard edge orientation.
Each nonzero summand in (
8
) corresponds to a dimer cover of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, also called
\sigma
.
When we project the edges of such a dimer cover
\sigma
to
{\mathcal{G}}
, it forms a
2n
-multiweb
\pi(\sigma)
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

So we can group the sum (
8
) as a sum over multiwebs:

\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}=\sum_{m\in\Omega_{2n}}\,\sum_{\sigma:\pi(\sigma)=m}\tilde{H}_{\sigma}

(9)

where

\tilde{H}_{\sigma}:=(-1)^{\sigma}\tilde{H}_{\sigma(1)\sigma(2)}\dots\tilde{H}_{\sigma(2M-1)\sigma(2M)}

and
where
\pi
is the projection from a dimer cover
\sigma
of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
to a
2n
-multiweb of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
To complete the proof it suffices to show that the inner sum in (
9
) is, up to a global sign independent of
m
, equal to
\mathrm{Tr}(m)
.

We identify the
2n
vertices of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
lying over a vertex
v\in{\mathcal{G}}
with the
2n
colors.
Each dimer cover
\sigma
of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
therefore corresponds to a
coloring of the half-edges of its projection
\pi(\sigma)
: a dimer
(u,i)\to(v,j)
projects to edge
uv
with colors
i
and
j

at its two endpoints.

At each vertex of
{\mathcal{G}}
, all colors appear in
\pi(\sigma)
, so each dimer cover
\sigma\in\pi^{-1}(m)

corresponds to a term in the expansion of
\mathrm{Tr}(m)
in (
4
) and (
5
), and conversely. Moreover the product of the entries in
\tilde{H}
given by
\sigma

corresponds to the product of factors in the trace: a dimer
(u,i)\to(v,j)
has weight
(J\phi_{uv})_{ij}
,
which is by definition the contraction along edge
uv
of the term in the codeterminants at
u
and
v
with entry

e_{i}
and
e_{j}
at
u,v
respectively.
So there is a weight-preserving bijection between terms in
\mathrm{Tr}(m)
and dimer covers in
\pi^{-1}(m)
.
It remains to compare their signs. More specifically, we need to compare the signs in (
8
) of two
general dimer covers
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, covering possibly different webs
m,m^{\prime}
, and
the signs in (
4
) and (
5
) of their corresponding colorings.

Let us take two dimer covers
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
and compare their signs. The union
\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}

is a union of even-length loops and doubled edges of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
; we can move from
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
by sliding dimers
one notch along each loop. So it suffices to consider the case that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
differ in a single loop
\gamma
.

We can assume at this point that
m
and
m^{\prime}
are simple, that is, have no multiple edges, by replacing
each edge with multiplicity by parallel edges in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Let
\gamma
be an even length loop in
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, with vertices
i_{1},\dots,i_{2\ell}
in cyclic order.
Along
\gamma
let
\sigma
be the pairing
\sigma=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}\{i_{3},i_{4}\}\dots\{i_{2\ell-1},i_{2\ell}\}

and
\sigma^{\prime}
be the pairing

\sigma^{\prime}=\{i_{2},i_{3}\}\{i_{4},i_{5}\},\dots,\{i_{2\ell},i_{1}\}
;
assume
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
agree off of
\gamma
.
Using the definition (
8
), the signature of
\sigma
is a constant (depending on indices not involved in
\gamma
)
times the signature of the permutation in line form
i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{2\ell}
, times
-1
per
pair of
\sigma
which is out of order. Likewise the signature of
\sigma^{\prime}
is the same constant times
the signature of
i_{2}i_{3}\dots i_{2\ell}i_{1}
, times
-1
per pair of
\sigma^{\prime}
which is out of order.
The ratio of signatures is then
-1
(for the cyclic permutation
(i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{2\ell})
of length
2\ell
)
times
-1
per pair of both
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
which is out of order,
that is,
(-1)^{d+1}
where
d
is the number of downward edges in
\gamma
, when
\gamma

is traversed cyclically in either direction.

Let us now compute the ratio of the signs of the colorings in (
4
).
Suppose first that
\gamma
in
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
projects to a loop
\pi(\gamma)
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Suppose
\pi(\gamma)
has vertices
v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{t}
of
{\mathcal{G}}
in order (
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are not necessarily distinct when
i\neq j
). The sign contribution at a vertex
v_{i}
is computed as follows.
As we traverse
\pi(\gamma)
, at vertex
v_{i}
let
k_{v_{i}}
be the number of edges in
\pi(\sigma)

to the left of
\pi(\gamma)
, that is, in the clockwise sector between the edge in
\pi(\gamma)
leading to
v_{i}
and the edge in
\pi(\gamma)
out from
v_{i}
.
Suppose the cilium at
v_{i}
is also in that sector. (See Figure
4
).

Figure 4:
At this vertex of
\gamma
we have
k_{v}=2
.

Then when moving from

\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
(sliding dimers by one notch along
\gamma
),
the sign change at
v_{i}
is
thus
(-1)^{k_{v_{i}}+1}
. If the cilium is to the right of
\pi(\gamma)
(not in the sector), the sign change is
(-1)^{k_{v_{i}}}
.

We claim that
\sum_{i=1}^{t}k_{v_{i}}
is always even. Consider a closed path in the plane starting just left of the first edge of
\pi(\gamma)

and following
\pi(\gamma)
all the way around, keeping just to its left. It crosses an even number of edges of
\pi(\sigma)
, counted with multiplicity,
since
\pi(\sigma)
has even degree at each vertex. But it also crosses exactly
k_{v_{i}}
edges of
\pi(\sigma)
while near vertex
v_{i}
, and so

\sum_{i=1}^{t}k_{v_{i}}
is even.

Taking the product of signs around
v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{t}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{t}k_{v_{i}}
is even, so the net sign change is

(-1)^{s}
where
s
is the number of cilia on the left of
\pi(\gamma)
.
By Lemma
1
this is

(-1)^{d+1}
where
d
is the number of downward edges in
\gamma
.
So the ratio of the signs of the colorings in (
4
) is equal to the ratio of the signs of terms in (
8
).

We have shown that sign changes due to changing colorings (in the traces) and sign changes due to changing signatures
(in the Pfaffian) agree for
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
. This completes the proof.

∎

A small generalization of Theorem
4
allows for the addition of edge weights.
Let
w:E\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
be a positive function on edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
, the
edge weight
(with
w_{uv}=w_{vu}
).
For a multiweb
m\in\Omega_{2n}
define its weight
w(m)
to be the product of its edge weights:
w(m)=\prod_{E}w_{e}^{m_{e}}
.
Define the weighted matrix
H=H(\Phi,w)
by

H_{uv}=\begin{cases}J\phi_{uv}w_{uv}&u\sim v\\
{\bf 0}&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Then we have

Theorem 5
.

We have
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{H}=\pm\sum_{m\in\Omega_{2n}}\mathrm{Tr}(m)w(m).

The proof is nearly identical to that of Theorem
4
, with the addition of the lift of the edge weights to edges of
\tilde{H}
.

5
The dimer model

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a planar graph and
w:E\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
a positive edge weight function.

A
dimer cover of
{\mathcal{G}}
, also known as
perfect matching of
{\mathcal{G}}
, is a set of edges covering all vertices exactly once.
Equivalently, it is a
1
-multiweb; the set of dimer covers is
\Omega_{1}
.
The weight of a dimer cover
m\in\Omega_{1}
is the product of its edge weights:

w(m)=\prod_{e:m_{e}=1}w_{e}
.
Let
Z_{d}=Z_{d}({\mathcal{G}})
, the
dimer partition function
, be the weighted sum of dimer covers,
Z_{d}=\sum_{m\in\Omega_{1}}w(m)
.

We define
\Phi_{K}
,
the
Kasteleyn connection
, be the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-connection (well-defined up to gauge equivalence)
in which each face has counterclockwise monodromy

J^{\ell-2}
, where
\ell
is the length of the face. For example if
{\mathcal{G}}
is a triangulation, then

\Phi_{K}
has monodromy
J
around every face. Generally for a loop
\gamma
, the monodromy will then
be
J^{A}
where
A
is the area enclosed, in terms of the number of triangles (quadrilateral faces count as area
2
,
pentagonal faces as area
3
, and so on).

The area
A(\gamma)
of a loop
\gamma
in
{\mathcal{G}}
can be related to its length
L(\gamma)
via the Euler characteristic:

A(\gamma)=L(\gamma)+2V_{enc}(\gamma)-2,

(10)

where
V_{enc}(\gamma)
is the number of vertices enclosed by the loop
\gamma
.

Theorem 6
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a planar graph with an even number
N
of vertices and
\Phi_{K}
be the
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
Kasteleyn connection.
Let
K=H(\Phi_{K})
be the associated
H
matrix with edge weights
w
.
Then

|\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{K}|=(Z_{d})^{2}.

Proof.

By (
6
) the trace of a
2
-web
m
is zero if any loop in
m
has odd area (and thus odd length),
because
\mathrm{tr}(J^{A(\gamma)})=0
if
A(\gamma)
is odd.
If all loops in
m
have even area, by (
6
) we have

\mathrm{Tr}(m)=(-1)^{c_{1}+N}\prod_{\gamma}-\mathrm{tr}(J^{A(\gamma)})=(-1)^{c_{1}+N}(-2)^{n_{0}}2^{n_{2}}

(11)

where the product is taken over all nontrivial loops
\gamma
(that is, not counting doubled edges) of
m
,
c_{1}
is the number of doubled edges,
n_{0}
is the number of loops of area
0\bmod 4
and
n_{2}
is the number of loops of area
2\bmod 4
.
If all loops are even then
V_{enc}(\gamma)
in (
10
) is always even and
A(\gamma)=L(\gamma)-2\bmod 4
by (
10
).

Now, consider a doubled edge to be a loop which has area
0
. Then taking the product over all loops in
m
, including doubled edges, and recalling that
N
is even, the sign in (
11
) is

(-1)^{c_{1}+n_{0}}=\prod_{\gamma}(-1)^{A(\gamma)/2+1}=\prod_{\gamma}(-1)^{L(\gamma)/2}=(-1)^{N/2}.

The trace is thus

\mathrm{Tr}(m)=(-1)^{N/2}2^{n_{0}+n_{2}}.
Note that the sign is independent of
m
. Summing over all
m\in\Omega_{2}
and applying Theorem
4

yields

|\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{K}|=\sum_{m\in\Omega_{2}}2^{n_{0}+n_{2}}.

Note however that the superposition of two dimers covers is a
2
-multweb with even-length loops, and the map from pairs of dimer covers to
such “even
2
-multiwebs” has preimage exactly
2^{\ell}
where
\ell=n_{0}+n_{2}
is the number of nontrivial loops in the
2
-multiweb.
Thus

(Z_{d})^{2}=\sum_{m\in\Omega_{2}}2^{n_{0}+n_{2}}=|\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{K}|.

∎

We define
Z_{dd}=(Z_{d})^{2}
; this is the partition function of the
double dimer model
, see
[
KW11
]
.

5.1
Rotation matrices

By perturbing the Kasteleyn
connection one can use it to study probabilistic properties of the double dimer model. One simple change one can make to
the Kasteleyn connection is to tensor with another connection which commutes with it.

Rotation matrices in
\mathrm{SL}(2)
are in
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
and also commute with
J
. We can thus tensor the Kasteleyn connection

\Phi_{K}
with an
S^{1}
-connection
\Theta
,
that is, an
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
-connection with connection matrices which are rotations

R_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}.

The resulting Pfaffian of
\tilde{K}=\tilde{K}(\Phi_{K}\otimes\Theta)
computes traces of
2
-multiwebs on
{\mathcal{G}}
where each loop
\gamma
now has weight
-\mathrm{tr}(J^{A}R_{\theta})
, where
R_{\theta}
is the monodromy of
\Theta
around
\gamma
and
A
is the area enclosed by

\gamma
(still in terms of the number of triangles: a face of length
k
has area
k-2
).

This gives trace

(2\cos\theta,-2\sin\theta,-2\cos\theta,2\sin\theta)

(12)

respectively
for loops of area respectively
(0,1,2,3)\bmod 4
, where
R_{\theta}
is the monodromy of
\Theta
around
\gamma
.

5.2
Double dimers on the annulus

From a probability perspective, if we want to study the double dimer model using the
2
-multiweb model,
we want only configurations in which all loops have even length; those with odd-length loops should have weight
0
.
This is only possible if in (
12
) we have
\theta=0
or
\theta=\pi
: the monodromy of
R_{\theta}
around odd-length
loops should be a multiple of
\pi
.
The remaining sign in (
12
) can then be absorbed into the total number of vertices as in Theorem
6
.

Suppose for example
{\mathcal{G}}
is embedded in a planar annulus, with inner face
\gamma_{0}
of length
\equiv 2\bmod 4
.
Equip
{\mathcal{G}}
with the
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
Kasteleyn connection;
then Theorem
6
computes the partition function
Z_{dd}=(Z_{d})^{2}
of the double-dimer model on
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Now tensor the Kasteleyn connection with a flat
R_{\pi}=-I
connection on the annulus, so that loops
\gamma
running around the annulus
now have monodromy
-J^{A}
, where
A
is the area between
\gamma
and
\gamma_{0}
.
A multiweb
m
has nonzero trace only when all loops, including those winding around the annulus, are even. Let
m_{0}
and
m_{2}
be the number of loops winding around the annulus
of length
0,2\bmod 4
respectively.
Let
n_{0},n_{2}
be the total number of loops of length
0,2\bmod 4
.
Then as in the proof of Theorem
6
the trace is

\mathrm{Tr}(m)=(-1)^{N/2}2^{n_{0}+n_{2}}(-1)^{m_{0}+m_{2}}.

Let
Z_{dd}(-1):=\mathrm{Pf}(\tilde{K})
be the associated partition function. The ratio

Z_{dd}(-1)/Z_{dd}
can be written

Z_{dd}(-1)/Z_{dd}={\mathbb{E}}[(-1)^{n}]
where
n
is the number of loops (necessarily of even length) separating
the two boundaries.

For a double dimer configuration on a planar graph we define a
spin
variable
\tau(f)\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
on each face
f
,
by defining it to be
0

on a fixed “basepoint” face
f_{0}
and changing by
1\bmod 2
when crossing any nontrivial loop. For the above annulus with external faces
f_{1},f_{2}
the spin correlation
{\mathbb{E}}[\tau(f_{1})\tau(f_{2})]
is computed to be

{\mathbb{E}}[\tau(f_{1})\tau(f_{2})]=Z_{dd}(-1)/Z_{dd}.

A similar calculation allows us to compute multiple spin correlations of the type

{\mathbb{E}}[\tau(f_{1})\dots\tau(f_{2k})]
for any
2k
given faces on the planar graph
{\mathcal{G}}
(assuming for simplicity that each face
f_{i}
has length
2\bmod 4
):
it suffices to compute the partition function for the Kasteleyn connection tensored
with the
\pm 1
connection with monodromy
-1
around each
f_{i}

and
+1
around all other faces.

5.3
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
and the Kasteleyn connection

Theorem
6
generalizes easily to
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a planar graph and
\Phi_{K}
be the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-Kasteleyn connection.
Let
K=H(\Phi_{K})
be the associated
H
matrix with edge weights
w
.
Then

|\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{K}|=(Z_{d})^{2n}.

Proof.

The matrix
\tilde{K}
is just a direct sum of
n
copies of the
\mathrm{Sp}(2)

\tilde{K}
matrix,
so this follows from Theorem
6
.
∎

Given a
2n
-multiweb
m
, we can compute its trace for the Kasteleyn connection
as a sum of colorings of its half-edges: Proposition
3
applies since
the Kasteleyn connection decomposes into a direct sum of
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
connections. A coloring of
m
decomposes
m
into a
union of
n
differently-colored
2
-multiwebs,
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
, one for each pair of complementary colors. Because we are using the Kasteleyn connection, each
m_{i}
has loops of even area (and hence even length) only. We call such loops
even
. The
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
-trace of
m_{i}
is given by
(-1)^{N/2}2^{l_{i}}
where
l_{i}
is the number of loops of
m_{i}
.
We have proved:

Theorem 8
.

For a
2n
-web
m
on a planar graph with the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
Kasteleyn connection, the trace of
m
is

\mathrm{Tr}(m)=(-1)^{nN/2}\sum_{c}2^{l(c)}w(m)

where the sum is over all decompositions
c
of
m
into colored even
2
-multiwebs, and
l
is the total number of loops in the decomposition
c
.

As an example see Figure
5
, with Kasteleyn matrix

K=\begin{pmatrix}0&-aI&cI&eJ\\
aI&0&-bI&fJ\\
-cI&bI&0&dJ\\
eJ&fJ&dJ&0\end{pmatrix}.

(13)

Figure 5:
For the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
on the left with edge weights
a,...,f
as shown, and Kasteleyn connection, we have

K
given by (
13
). The Pfaffian of
\tilde{K}
is

(ad+be+cf)^{4}
. The
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
multiweb
m
in the second panel can be decomposed into colored loops in four ways (2 of which are shown; the other two
are obtained by reversing colors). Counting
2^{\text{\# loops}}
(not counting doubled edges), these configurations have weight
2
and
4

respectively, for a total trace of
\mathrm{Tr}(m)=2(2+4)=12
. The coefficient of
a^{2}bcd^{2}ef
in
\mathrm{Pf}\tilde{K}
is indeed
12
.

5.4
U(n)
connections

As in the
\mathrm{Sp}(2)
case, we can perturb the Kasteleyn
connection by tensoring with another connection which commutes with it.

A matrix
A\in\mathrm{Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
which is both symplectic and commutes with
J
is orthogonal:

J=A^{t}JA=A^{t}AJ
implies
A^{t}A=I
. Conversely the intersection of
\mathrm{Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
and
O(2n,{\mathbb{R}})

consists of the symplectic matrices which commute with
J
. This group is isomorphic to
U(n)
, the unitary group.
The embedding
\rho
of
U(n)
in the symplectic group is as follows. Given
M\in U(n)
, we define

\rho(M)=\begin{pmatrix}\Re(M)&\Im(M)\\
-\Im(M)&\Re(M)\end{pmatrix}.

By abuse of notation we refer to this subgroup as
U(n)
.

Consequently it is natural to consider a planar graph
{\mathcal{G}}
with a
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
-Kasteleyn connection tensored with a
U(n)
connection.
Applying Theorem
4
to the connection with perturbed traces gives information about the shape of a random
2n
-multiweb.
We just need to see how the trace of a
2n
-multiweb changes under the perturbation.
For
n>1
this is complicated by the fact that the topology of a
2n
web is typically itself complicated.

We leave this as a potentially interesting problem.

Problem 1
.

Study the interacting
2n
-dimer model in the presence of a
U(n)
connection.

One result for
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
is discussed in the next section.

5.5
4
-dimer model on an annulus

Let us consider the simplest case in which
a graph
{\mathcal{G}}
is embedded in a planar annulus, with inner face
\gamma_{0}
of length
\equiv 2\bmod 4
.
Equip
{\mathcal{G}}
with the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
Kasteleyn connection.
Tensor this connection with a flat connection with monodromy
R\in U(2)
around the annulus.

By Theorem
20
, a reduced
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-web on
{\mathcal{G}}
has components which are single loops or “doubled loops”.
So the trace of a
4
-multiweb on
{\mathcal{G}}
as a function of
R
is a linear combination of products of
the trace of a single loop and the trace of a doubled loop.

The general matrix
R\in U(2)
has the form

\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)e^{i\alpha}&\sin(\theta)e^{i\beta}\\
-\sin(\theta)e^{-i\beta}e^{i\epsilon}&\cos(\theta)e^{-i\alpha}e^{i\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}

for real
\theta,\alpha,\beta,\epsilon
.

For this
R
, the trace of an odd single loop is
0
; a short calculation shows that the trace of an odd doubled-loop is

1-\cos(2\alpha-\epsilon)+2\cos(\epsilon)-2\cos^{2}(\alpha-\frac{\epsilon}{2})\cos(2\theta).

(14)

Likewise the trace of an even single loop is

2(\cos\alpha+\cos(\alpha-\epsilon))\cos\theta

(15)

and the trace of an even doubled-loop is

1+\cos(2\alpha-\epsilon)+2\cos(\epsilon)+2\cos^{2}(\alpha-\frac{\epsilon}{2})\cos(2\theta).

(16)

In order to have no odd loops we set the odd doubled-loop trace (
14
) to zero, by setting

\cos 2\theta=\frac{1-\cos(2\alpha-\epsilon)+2\cos(\epsilon)}{2\cos^{2}(\alpha-\frac{\epsilon}{2})}.

Then the trace of a single even loop (
15
) becomes
\pm(2+2\cos\epsilon)
and the trace of a doubled even loop (
16
) becomes
2+4\cos\epsilon
,
and in particular these are independent of
\beta,\alpha
.

Therefore there is no loss in generality in setting
\alpha=0=\beta
, in which case
\theta=0
or
\theta=\pi
and

R=\pm\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{i\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

Suppose
\theta=0
, so that the weight of a single loop is
2+2\cos\epsilon
.
This is
1
plus half the weight of a doubled loop.

In terms of enumeration,
we do not have enough information to count both the number of single loops and the number of doubled loops, since their traces only depend on one parameter
\epsilon
.
If we add the supplementary skein relation of Figure

6
, however,

Figure 6:
A “supplementary” skein relation on an annulus.

which replaces each single loop with a linear combination of the empty configuration
and
1/2
of a doubled loop,
then every configuration can be reduced to a nonnegative linear combination of
doubled loops:

Z_{4}(\epsilon)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}C_{k}(2+4\cos\epsilon)^{k}

where
C_{k}
is the sum of traces of configurations resolving to
k
doubled loops.
By varying
\epsilon
we can extract
C_{k}
for each
k
.

6
\rm{Sp(4)}
webs and representations

6.1
Multiwebs and webs

A
2n
-web
is a
2n
-valent graph, embedded in a disk or on a surface, possibly with some boundary vertices of degree
1
on the boundary of the disk or surface.

For a graph
{\mathcal{G}}
embedded in an oriented surface, to each
2n
-multiweb
m
is associated a
2n
-web
\hat{m}
,
with the same vertices as
m
and such that an edge of
m
of multiplicity
m_{e}
is replaced with
m_{e}
parallel edges connecting
its endpoints. The web
\hat{m}
inherits a ribbon structure from that of
m
,
as well as cilia and edge orientations.

While multiwebs play a combinatorial role in this paper, webs themselves are representation-theoretic objects, defining invariants in tensor
products of representations, see
[
Kup96
]
.
Skein relations on webs, which a priori arise from the underlying representation theory, give rise to analogous relations for multiwebs;
these are relations between traces of multiwebs.
We focus here primarily
on the case of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
webs, whose skein relations were studied in
[
Kup96
]
.

6.2
Definition of the
\rm{Sp(4)}
web category

The connection between graphs and the representations of a Lie group is usefully described using the language of category theory.

A category is a collection of objects and morphisms, where a morphism relates two objects called the source and the target of the morphism. In a graphical category, a morphism is given by a formal linear combination of graphs with the same boundary points, and an object is given by a tuple of boundary points. We read a graph vertically as a morphism, which means that the tuple of boundary points beneath the graph is the source, and the tuple of boundary points above the graph is the target. Composition of morphisms corresponds to vertical stacking of graphs.

If a category
\mathbf{C}
is equipped with a tensor:

\bigotimes:\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, such that
\bigotimes
is associative and for which
there is an identity object, then we call
\mathbf{C}
a monoidal category or a tensor category. A graphical category can be equipped with a tensor simply by putting graphs side by side. To be more precise, the tensor product of objects is horizontal concatenation of tuples of boundary points of graphs. The tensor product of morphisms is horizontal concatenation of graphs. The identity object is given by the empty diagram, which can be seen as a tuple of no boundary points.

For example, consider

(18)

Remark 11
.

In the
Sp(4)
web category, the diagrams are read vertically and the tetravalent vertex is well-defined without reference to cilia, because there is already a natural order of inputs and outputs. When we study
4
-multiwebs or
4
-webs on a planar graph, however,
cilia are introduced to indicate how diagrams are read locally around each vertex. See Section
6.3
below.

(22)

Proof.

Apply Equation (
18
) to rewrite the webs with tetravalent vertices as linear combinations of webs with double edges, then apply skein relations in Definition
1
.
∎

A morphism in the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category is a linear combination of trivalent graphs. These graphs are called
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-webs. Note that this is different from our notion of
4
-webs above. However, these notions of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-web and
4
-webs are related by Definition
10
.

To be more precise, taking any
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-web
\mathcal{I}\in{\Hom}(\ObjOne^{\otimes n},\mathbb{R})
, using Definition
10
we can write
\mathcal{I}
as a linear combination of tetravalent graphs, each of which is a
4
-web.

Definition 13
.

A
4
-web on a surface is said to be
reduced
if it has no contractible faces of degree
0,1,2
or
3
.

Note that from Proposition
12
, a web with a faces of degree
<4
can be written as a linear combination of webs with fewer faces. This gives us a confluent reduction procedure for
4
-webs.

6.3
Rotation of webs

For any web
\mathcal{W}\in{\Hom}(\ObjOne^{\otimes n},\ObjOne^{\otimes m})
,
we can associate to
\mathcal{W}
a web
\mathcal{W}_{0}\in{\Hom}(\ObjOne^{\otimes(m+n)},\mathbb{R})
by the following.
First we have

These
\mathcal{W}_{k}
can be identified with each other by rotation of the diagrams.
These rotations
\mathcal{W}_{k}
of web
\mathcal{W}

correspond to different choices of cilia, since they rotate the inputs.
Any
\mathcal{W}_{k}
can be indicated by a web in a disk with a choice of cilium.

6.4
The representation category of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)

Now we recall some basic representation theory of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
:

The defining
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
representation
V
is
4
dimensional, with basis vectors:

v_{(1,0)}=e_{1},\qquad v_{(-1,1)}=e_{2},\qquad v_{(1,-1)}=e_{4},\quad\text{and}\quad v_{(-1,0)}=-e_{3},

where the subscripts of the vectors are weights in the representation. The defining representation
V
is the first fundamental representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
, also denoted
V_{(1,0)}
.

We have
V\wedge V\cong V_{(0,1)}\oplus\mathbb{R}
, where
V_{(0,1)}
is the second fundamental representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
and has dimension
5
. We can write down the basis vectors of
V_{(0,1)}
as vectors in
V\wedge V
:

v_{(0,1)}=e_{1}\wedge e_{2},\qquad v_{(2,-1)}=e_{1}\wedge e_{4},\qquad v_{(0,0)}=e_{2}\wedge e_{4}-e_{1}\wedge e_{3},

v_{(-2,1)}=-e_{2}\wedge e_{3},\quad\text{and}\quad v_{(0,-1)}=-e_{4}\wedge e_{3}.

Definition 2
.

The
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
fundamental representation category, denoted by
\Fund(\rm{Sp(4)})
, is a monoidal category, whose objects are generated by the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
fundamental representations
\{V_{(1,0)},V_{(0,1)}\}
, and whose morphisms are
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
homomorphisms between
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
representations.

The
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
web category
\CDDC
is essentially the same as the
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
fundamental representation category
\Fund(\mathrm{Sp}(4))
, by recalling an equivalence between the two categories in the following theorem by Kuperberg.

Theorem 14

(
[
Kup96
, Theorem 5.1 and 6.9]
)
.

There is an equivalence of monoidal categories by the following functor

\Phi:\CDDC\rightarrow\Fund(\rm{Sp(4)})

such that
\Phi(\ObjOne)=V_{(1,0)}
,
\Phi(\ObjTwo)=V_{(0,1)}
.

Furthermore, one can write down the image of an
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-web under the functor
\Phi
as a homomorphism between
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
representations, see
[
Bod22
]
.
Since
\bigwedge^{4}V
is
1
-dimensional with basis
e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}\wedge e_{4}
,
for
w\in\bigwedge^{4}V
, we denote by
|w|
the element
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
w=r(e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}\wedge e_{4})
.

Proposition 15

(
[
Bod22
, Section 3]
)
.

The functor
\Phi
sends the generating morphisms in
\CDDC
to morphisms in
\Fund(\rm{Sp(4)})
by the following.

\displaystyle\Phi\bigg(\hbox to43.34pt{\vbox to36.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.33305pt\lower-11.24614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}

We can show that
\Phi(\textbf{det})
takes a tensor product of
4
vectors in
V
to their wedge product in
\wedge^{4}V\cong\mathbb{R}
by explicit vector calculations from Theorem
14
. Similar arguments work for
\Phi(\textbf{codet})
.
∎

Proposition 17
.

We have

\displaystyle\Phi(\beta_{\ObjOne,\ObjOne}):V\otimes V

\displaystyle\rightarrow V\otimes V

\displaystyle u\otimes v

\displaystyle\mapsto v\otimes u.

By direct calculations,

s\wedge t\wedge u\wedge v=(u\cdot Js)(v\cdot Jt)-(t\cdot Js)(v\cdot Ju)-(v\cdot Js)(u\cdot Jt).

Comparing parts we are left with

\Phi\left(\hbox to64.01pt{\vbox to38.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.00238pt\lower-43.86986pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -40.96 -54.61 C -32.77 -27.31 -27.31 -13.65 -13.65 -13.65 C 0 -13.65 5.46 -27.31 13.65 -54.61}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=4.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=8.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 40.96 -54.61 C 32.77 -27.31 27.31 -13.65 13.65 -13.65 C 0 -13.65 -5.46 -27.31 -13.65 -54.61}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 40.96 -54.61 C 32.77 -27.31 27.31 -13.65 13.65 -13.65 C 0 -13.65 -5.46 -27.31 -13.65 -54.61}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right):s\otimes t\otimes u\otimes v\mapsto(u\cdot Js)(v\cdot Jt)

which is equivalent to the statement.
∎

7
4
-webs on simple surfaces

We study the space of closed
4
-webs on a simple surface.
Sikora and Westbury
[
SW06
]
proved that reduced
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-webs form a basis for the space of
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
webs on any surface. We classify all closed reduced
4
-webs on an annulus, a torus, and a pair of pants, and therefore give a basis description for the space of closed
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-webs on the corresponding simple surface.

7.1
Annulus

We consider here the case where the graph
\mathcal{G}
is embedded on an annulus.

.

4

.

Theorem 20
.

By use of skein relations (
20
), (
21
), (
22
), and (
23
), any
4
-web
on an annulus with a flat
\mathrm{Sp}(4)
-local system can be reduced to a unique linear combination of disjoint unions of noncontractible 1-loops and 2-loops.

Proof.

For a connected tetravalent graph
\mathcal{G}
on a sphere which is not a cycle, we have (by Euler characteristic)

8=3n_{1}+2n_{2}+1n_{3}+0n_{4}-1n_{5}+\dots+(4-k)n_{k}+\dots

where
n_{k}
is the number of faces with
k
edges.

When a tetravalent graph
{\mathcal{G}}
is embedded on an annulus, at most two faces contain boundary components. So there is at least one contractible monogon face or one contractible bigon face or two contractible triangle faces.

Denote the number of vertices of
\mathcal{G}
by
V
. We finish the proof by induction on
V
.

•

When there is a contractible monogon face, we can reduce
V
by using relation (
20
).

•

When there is a contractible bigon face such that the two vertices of the bigon are different, we can reduce
V
by using relation (
21
).

•

When there is a contractible bigon face such that the two vertices of the bigon are the same (and no contractible monogon), we know that
\mathcal{G}
is a 2-cycle.

•

When there is a contractible triangle face such that all the three vertices of the triangle are different, we can reduce
V
by using relation (
22
).

•

When there is a contractible triangle face such that two of the three vertices of the bigon are the same, we can reduce
V
by using relation (
23
).

Applying the above inductive step reduces the number of vertices in
\mathcal{G}
and possibly disconnects
\mathcal{G}
, unless
\mathcal{G}
is itself a 2-cycle. We can continue applying the inductive step to each component until every component is a cycle or 2-cycle.

∎

7.2
Torus

We consider here the case where the graph
\mathcal{G}
is embedded on a torus.

Proposition 21
.

Any reduced connected
4
-web on a torus is either a noncontractible cycle, a noncontractible 2-cycle, or a square grid
on the torus (which can be described by a quotient of the square grid on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by a sublattice of translations in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
).

Proof.

Consider a reduced
4
-web
\mathcal{W}
on a torus. If there is a noncontractible simple loop
\gamma
on the torus,
such that
\gamma
and
\mathcal{W}

don’t intersect, then we can cut the torus along
\gamma
, and
\mathcal{W}
becomes a reduced web on an annulus. So
\mathcal{W}
is either a noncontractible cycle or a noncontractible 2-cycle in this case.

If
\mathcal{W}
intersects all the noncontractible loops, then
\mathcal{W}
doesn’t have a non-reducible bigon face.
So by skein relations (
20
), (
21
), (
22
), and (
23
),
\mathcal{W}
doesn’t have any face with less than
4
edges. On the other hand, since the Euler characteristic of a torus is equal to
0
,
\mathcal{W}
doesn’t have any face with more than
4
edges. So
\mathcal{W}
is a square grid on a torus, which is necessarily
a quotient of the standard square grid on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by a sublattice of translations in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
∎

7.3
Pair of pants

Let
\Sigma
be a pair of pants, that is, a sphere with three punctures.
There are two types of special reduced
4
-webs on a pair of pants, which we denote
W_{\eta}
and
W_{\xi}
, which we now describe.

Let
\eta=(a,b)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, with
\eta\neq(0,0)
. We construct the dual graph of a reduced web
W_{\eta}
on
\Sigma
as follows.
Choose
S_{1}
to be a grid point on the square grid. Let

\overrightarrow{S_{1}P}=(a,b),\quad\overrightarrow{PS_{2}}=(b,-a),\quad\overrightarrow{S_{2}Q}=(-a,-b),\quad\overrightarrow{QS_{1}}=(-b,a).

From the square
PS_{1}QS_{2}
, glue sides
PS_{1}
with
PS_{2}
and
QS_{1}
with
QS_{2}
as shown, putting punctures at the corners, to form a 3-punctured sphere
\Sigma
.
The dual graph of the image of the square grid is a
4
-web
W_{\eta}
on
\Sigma
with a monogon face at
P
and
Q
and a bigon face at
S_{1}=S_{2}
, and all other faces are quads.

\displaystyle\text{Dual graph of }W_{(-2,3)}:\hbox to116.19pt{\vbox to117.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.87503pt\lower-15.39932pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000B3}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

Let
\xi
be a hexagon
P_{1}P_{2}\dots P_{6}
with vertices in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, with right angles at
P_{1},P_{3},P_{5}
and

\displaystyle|P_{1}P_{2}|

\displaystyle=|P_{1}P_{6}|

\displaystyle|P_{3}P_{2}|

\displaystyle=|P_{3}P_{4}|

\displaystyle|P_{5}P_{4}|

\displaystyle=|P_{5}P_{6}|,

see Figure
7
.

Figure 7:
A hexagon
\xi
.

Glue sides
P_{1}P_{6}
and
P_{1}P_{2}
, sides
P_{3}P_{2}
and
P_{3}P_{4}
, and sides
P_{5}P_{4}
and
P_{5}P_{6}

to form a closed genus
0
surface
\Sigma_{\xi}
tiled with squares, with cone angles
\pi/2
at
P_{1},P_{3},P_{5}
and angle
5\pi/2
at the common image
of
P_{2},P_{4},P_{6}
. The dual graph of the image of the square grid is a
4
-web
W_{\xi}
on
\Sigma_{\xi}
with a monogon face surrounding
each of
P_{1},P_{3},P_{5}
, a pentagonal face around
P_{2}=P_{4}=P_{6}
, and other faces of degree
4
.

Lemma 3
.

Consider a quadrangulation of the sphere with unit Euclidean squares and with
n
cone points
v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}
, where
v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n-1}
have positive curvatures. Suppose the shortest geodesic from
v_{n}
to
v_{i}
is
l_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
. Then
l_{i}
and
l_{j}
only intersect at
v_{n}
if
i\neq j
.

Proof.

Suppose
l_{i}
and
l_{j}
intersect at
u\neq v_{n}
.

v_{n}
v_{i}
v_{j}
u
l_{i}
l_{j}
\alpha
\beta

Consider the geodesic distances
D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},u)
and
D_{l_{j}}(v_{n},u)
.
If the distances are not equal, suppose
D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},u)>D_{l_{j}}(v_{n},u)
, then
D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},v_{i})=D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},u)+D_{l_{i}}(u,v_{i})>D_{l_{j}}(v_{n},u)+D_{l_{i}}(u,v_{i})
, which is contradicted to
l_{i}
being the shortest geodesic from
v_{n}
to
v_{i}
.

Since
u\neq v_{n}
,
u
is either not a cone point or is a cone point with positive curvature. So
\alpha+\beta<2\pi
, which means that
\alpha<\pi
or
\beta<\pi
. If
D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},u)=D_{l_{j}}(v_{n},u)
, suppose
\alpha<\pi
, then there exist a geodesic from
v_{n}
to
v_{i}
whose length is less than
D_{l_{j}}(v_{n},u)+D_{l_{i}}(u,v_{i})=D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},u)+D_{l_{i}}(u,v_{i})=D_{l_{i}}(v_{n},v_{i})
. This contradicts the facts that

l_{i}
is a shortest geodesic from
v_{n}
to
v_{i}
.
∎

Theorem 22
.

A reduced
4
-web on a pair of pants is a union of a collection of cycles and 2-cycles and at most one of the webs of type
W_{\xi}
or
W_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Consider a connected reduced
4
-web
\mathcal{W}
on
\Sigma
.
If
\mathcal{W}
has a face containing more than one boundary component, it is a
1
-loop or
2
-loop, by Theorem
20
.
Otherwise, if each face of
\mathcal{W}
contains at most one boundary component, we have (by Euler characteristic)

8=3n_{1}+2n_{2}+1n_{3}+0n_{4}-1n_{5}+\dots+(4-k)n_{k}+\dots

(24)

where
n_{k}
is the number of faces with
k
edges.

By relations (
20
), (
21
), (
22
), and (
23
), any contractible face of
\mathcal{W}
has at least
4
edges. Then by equation (
24
),
\mathcal{W}
falls into one of the following two cases.

•

\mathcal{W}
has
3
noncontractible monogon faces,
1
contractible pentagon face, and contractible square faces.

•

\mathcal{W}
has
2
noncontractible monogon faces,
1
noncontractible bigon face, and contractible square faces.

Take the dual graph
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
of
\mathcal{W}
. It is a quadrangulation
\rm{Q}
of
S^{2}
. Its cone points (for the Euclidean metric in which all faces are unit squares) are given by vertices of
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
which are dual to the monogon faces, bigon faces, or pentagon faces of
\mathcal{W}
.

The cone point dual to the monogon face has cone angle
\cfrac{\pi}{2}
, and thus curvature there
\cfrac{3\pi}{2}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
E
be the quadrangulation of
\mathbb{C}
by the unit square grid.

Suppose
\mathcal{W}
has
2
noncontractible monogon faces,
1
noncontractible bigon face, and contractible square faces.
Then
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
has three cone points with positive curvatures, adding up to
4\pi
.
Denote singular vertices of
\rm{Q}
by
v_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. Suppose
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have curvature
\cfrac{3\pi}{2}
, and
v_{3}
has curvature
\pi
.
Consider the shortest geodesics from
v_{3}
to
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. By Lemma
3
, we know that the geodesics are non-intersecting except for at
v_{3}
. Since
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
is a quadrangulation of the sphere, we can cut the sphere open along the geodesics.
What remains unfolds to a quadrilateral with two opposite vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
of angle
\pi/2
, each with two adjacent sides of the same length. This is a square.
So
{\mathcal{W}}
is given by
W_{\xi}
in this case.

Suppose
\mathcal{W}
has
3
noncontractible monogon faces,
1
contractible pentagon face, and contractible square faces.
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
has four cone points,
3
of which have positive curvatures.
Denote singular vertices of
\rm{Q}
by
v_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
. Suppose
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
, and
v_{3}
have curvature
\cfrac{3\pi}{2}
, and
v_{4}
has curvature
-\pi/2
.

Consider the shortest geodesics from
v_{4}
to
v_{1},v_{2},
and
v_{3}
. By Lemma
3
, we know that the geodesics are non-intersecting except for at
v_{4}
. Since
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
is a quadrangulation of the sphere, we can cut the sphere open along the geodesics. Unfolding, we get a hexagon of type
\xi
above.
So
{\mathcal{W}}
is given by
W_{\xi}
in this case.
∎

8
Sp(2n)
webs

8.1
Definition of
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web category

Definition 3

(
[
BERT21
, Definition 1.1]
)
.

The category of
\rm{Sp(2n)}
webs, denoted by
\textbf{Web}(\rm{Sp(2n)})
, is a monoidal category, whose objects are generated by objects
\{\ObjOne,\ObjTwo,\dots,\ObjN\}
, and whose morphisms are generated by the following graphs, where the edge labeling
(not to be confused with the edge multiplicity, which we denote in bold) agrees with the labeling of the corresponding boundary points:

(25)

Theorem 23

(
[
BERT21
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

There is an equivalence of monoidal categories

\displaystyle\Phi:\textbf{Web}(Sp(2n))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\textbf{Fund}(Sp(2n))

\displaystyle\ObjK

\displaystyle\mapsto V_{\varpi_{k}}

where
V_{\varpi_{k}}
is the
k
-th fundamental representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
.

8.2
2n
-valent vertex in the
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
Web Category

Definition 24
.

Define the crossing
\beta_{\ObjOne,\ObjOne}\in{\Hom}(\ObjOne\otimes\ObjOne,\ObjOne\otimes\ObjOne)

Proposition 25

(
[
BERT21
, Porism 5.4]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\Phi(\beta_{\ObjOne,\ObjOne}):V_{\varpi_{1}}\otimes V_{\varpi_{1}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{\varpi_{1}}\otimes V_{\varpi_{1}}

\displaystyle u\otimes v

\displaystyle\mapsto v\otimes u

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in P_{2n}}(-1)^{K_{\alpha}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}M_{\alpha},

where
P_{2n}=\{((i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}),...,(i_{n},j_{n})):i_{k}<j_{k},\ i_{k}<i_{k+1},\ 1\leq i_{k},j_{k}\leq 2n\}
and
M_{\alpha}\in\Hom(\ObjOne^{\otimes 2n},\mathbb{R})
such that:

\displaystyle\Phi(M_{\alpha}):\qquad\qquad\qquad{V_{\varpi_{1}}}^{\otimes 2n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n}

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{k=1}^{n}(v_{j_{k}}\cdot Jv_{i_{k}})

K_{\alpha}
is the number of crossings in
M_{\alpha}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Now we prove Theorem
2
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

V_{\varpi_{1}}
is the defining representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
, with basis
e_{1},e_{2},...,e_{2n}
.
First, we show that

\Phi(\textbf{det})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes e_{2n})=1.

(27)

For
\alpha\in P_{2n}
, we know that

\Phi(M_{\alpha})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes e_{2n})=0,

unless
\alpha=\alpha_{0}:=((1,n+1),(2,n+2),...,(n,2n))
. We also know that

K_{\alpha_{0}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}.

What’s more,
e_{k+n}\cdot Je_{k}=-1,
for
1\leq k\leq n
. So

\displaystyle\Phi(\textbf{det})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes e_{2n})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{K_{\alpha_{0}}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}\Phi(M_{\alpha_{0}})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes e_{2n})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{n}(e_{k+n}\cdot Je_{k})=(-1)^{n(n+1)}=1

Second, we show that

\Phi(\textbf{det})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k}\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n})=-\Phi(\textbf{det})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes v_{k}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n}).

(28)

Denote
\sigma_{k,k+1}:={id_{\ObjOne}}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes\beta_{\ObjOne,\ObjOne}\otimes{id_{\ObjOne}}^{\otimes(n-k-1)}\in\Hom(\ObjOne^{\otimes 2n},\ObjOne^{\otimes 2n})
.
On the one hand,

\displaystyle\Phi(\textbf{det}\circ\sigma_{k,k+1})(

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes v_{k}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n})=

\displaystyle=(\Phi(\textbf{det})\circ\Phi(\sigma_{k,k+1}))(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes v_{k}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\textbf{det})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k}\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n}).

On the other hand, by Proposition
26
,

\displaystyle\textbf{det}\circ\sigma_{k,k+1}=\sum_{\alpha\in P_{2n}}(-1)^{K_{\alpha}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}(M_{\alpha}\circ\sigma_{k,k+1})=-\sum_{\tilde{\alpha}\in P_{2n}}(-1)^{K_{\tilde{\alpha}}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}M_{\tilde{\alpha}}.

This tells us that
\textbf{det}\circ\sigma_{k,k+1}=-\textbf{det}
, which implies

\Phi(\textbf{det}\circ\sigma_{k,k+1})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes v_{k}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n})=-\Phi(\textbf{det})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{k+1}\otimes v_{k}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n}).

Combining (
27
) and (
28
) with linearity we have

\Phi(\textbf{det})(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdot\cdot\cdot\otimes v_{2n})=|v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdot\cdot\cdot\wedge v_{2n}|
.
∎

\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
multiwebs on an annulus

Lemma 4
.

When over and under crossings are identified, any link diagram on an annulus is isotopic to a disjoint union of contractible circles and elements in
\{\mathcal{K}_{k}:k\geq 1\}
. Suppose that there is a path from one boundary circle to the other, which intersects the link
k
times.
Then the link is a disjoint union of contractible circles and
elements
\mathcal{K}_{k_{i}},k_{i}\geq 1
, such that
\sum k_{i}\leq k
.

Proof.

An elementary application of the Reidemeister theorem.
∎

Proof.

Resolve the crossing by Equation (
26
) to deduce the first equation, then apply Equation (
25
) to deduce the second equation.
∎

Theorem 29
.

Any
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web on an annulus can be written as a linear combination of disjoint unions of elements in
\{\mathcal{K}_{k}:1\leq k\leq n\}
.

Proof.

First, for any
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web
\mathcal{W}
, one can apply Relation (
25
) to write
\mathcal{W}
as a linear combination of webs with edges labeled by
1
and
2
. Furthermore, edges labeled
2
can be replaced by linear combinations of planar matchings and crossings by Relation (
26
). In conclusion,
\mathcal{W}
is equal to a linear combination of webs with only edges
labeled
1
and crossings.

By Proposition
26
, an
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web with edges labeled
1
and crossings can be seen as a link diagram, where over and under crossings are identified. Then by Lemma
4
,
\mathcal{W}
is a linear combination of disjoint unions of contractible circles and elements in
\{\mathcal{K}_{k}:k\geq 1\}
.

Use
\mathbf{D}_{<m}
to denote a linear combination of diagrams which are disjoint unions of contractible circles and elements in
\{\mathcal{K}_{k}:1\leq k\leq m-1\}
. We now show that for
m>n
,
\mathcal{K}_{m}
can be written as
\mathbf{D}_{<m}
by Definition
3
and Lemma
5
.
Apply Relation (
26
) to resolve the crossing of
\mathcal{K}_{m}
that is closest to the puncture of the annulus, we know that
\mathcal{K}_{m}
can be written as the following:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\mathbf{D}_{<m}

Since
m>n
, we can apply Lemma
5
consecutively
n
times, and write
\mathcal{K}_{m}
as a sum of
\mathbf{D}_{<m}
and an
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web with an edge labelled by
(n+1)
.

By Definition
3
, we know that any
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web with an edge labelled by
(n+1)
is equal to
0
. So
\mathcal{K}_{m}
is equal to
\mathbf{D}_{<m}
when
m>n
. By induction, any
\mathrm{Sp}(2n)
web
\mathcal{W}
can be written as
\mathbf{D}_{<(n+1)}
. Then by Definition
3
, a contractible circle can be evaluated as a scalar, so
\mathcal{W}
can be written as a linear combination of disjoint unions of elements in
\{\mathcal{K}_{k}:1\leq k\leq n\}
.
∎
